Dynamic Type Inference for Gradual Hindley–Milner Typing DOI: 10.1145/3290331 Journal: PACMPL Volume: 3 POPL 18 1 CCS: Theory of computation Operational semantics CCS: Software and its engineering Functional languages CCS: Software and its engineering Dynamic analysis CCS: Software and its engineering Polymorphism
Yusuke Miyazaki, Taro Sekiyama, Atsushi Igarashi

Abstract
. Garcia and Cimini study a type inference problem for the ITGL, an implicitly and gradually typed language with let-polymorphism, and develop a sound and complete inference algorithm for it. Soundness and completeness mean that, if the algorithm succeeds, the input term can be translated to a well-typed term of an explicitly typed blame calculus by cast insertion and vice versa. However, in general, there are many possible translations depending on how type variables that were left undecided by static type inference are instantiated with concrete static types. Worse, the translated terms may behave differently—some evaluate to values but others raise blame. In this paper, we propose and formalize a new blame calculus λ B DTI \lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}} that avoids such divergence as an intermediate language for the ITGL. A main idea is to allow a term to contain type variables (that have not been instantiated during static type inference) and defer instantiation of these type variables to run time. We introduce dynamic type inference (DTI) into the semantics of λ B DTI \lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}} so that type variables are instantiated along reduction. The DTI-based semantics not only avoids the divergence described above but also is sound and complete with respect to the semantics of fully instantiated terms in the following sense: if the evaluation of a term succeeds (i.e., terminates with a value) in the DTI-based semantics, then there is a fully instantiated version of the term that also succeeds in the explicitly typed blame calculus and vice versa. Finally, we prove the gradual guarantee, which is an important correctness criterion of a gradually typed language, for the ITGL.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Gradual Typing

Statically and dynamically typed languages have complementary strengths.
On the one hand, static typing provides early detection of bugs, but
the enforced programming style can be too constrained, especially when
the type system is not very expressive. On the other hand, dynamic
typing is better suited for rapid prototyping and fast adaption to
changing requirements, but error detection is deferred to run time.

There has been much work to integrate static and dynamic typing in a single
programming
language
(
Abadi
et al. 1991
;
Cartwright and
Fagan 1991
;
Thatte 1990
;
Bracha and
Griswold 1993
;
Flanagan and
Felleisen 1999
;
Siek and Taha 2006
;
Tobin-Hochstadt and Felleisen 2008
)
.

Gradual typing

(
Siek and Taha 2006
)
is a framework to enable seamless code evolution from a fully dynamically typed program to a fully statically typed one within a single language.
The notion of gradual typing has been applied to various language features such
as objects
(
Siek and Taha 2007
)
,
generics
(
Ina and Igarashi 2011
)
,
effects
(
Bañados Schwerter et al. 2014
;
Bañados Schwerter et al. 2016
)
,
ownership
(
Sergey and Clarke 2012
)
,
parametric polymorphism
(
Ahmed
et al. 2011
;
Igarashi
et al. 2017
;
Xie
et al. 2018
)

and so on.
More recently, even methodologies to “gradualize” existing statically typed
languages systematically, i.e., to generate gradually typed languages, are also
studied
(
Garcia
et al. 2016
;
Cimini and Siek 2016
;
Cimini and Siek 2017
)
.

The key notions in gradual typing are
the dynamic type
and

type consistency
. The dynamic type, denoted by
\star
, is the
type for the dynamically typed code. For instance, a function that
accepts an argument of type
\star
can use it in any way and the
function can be applied to any value. So, both
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}+2)\,3

and
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}+2)\,\textsf{{true}}
are well-typed programs in a gradually
typed language. To formalize such loose static typechecking, a type
consistency relation, denoted by
\sim
, on types replaces some use
of type equality. In the typing rule for applications, the function
argument type and the type of an actual argument are required not to
be equal but to be consistent; also, both
\star\sim\textsf{{int}}
and

\star\sim\textsf{{bool}}
hold, making the two terms above well typed.

The semantics of a gradually typed language is usually defined by a
“cast-inserting” translation into an intermediate language with
explicit casts, which perform run-time typechecking.
For example, the two examples above can be translated into terms of the blame calculus
(
Wadler and
Findler 2009
)
as follows.

\displaystyle(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}+2)\,3

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow

\displaystyle(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})+2)\,(3:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)

\displaystyle(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}+2)\,\textsf{{true}}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow

\displaystyle(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})+2)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)

Here,
\rightsquigarrow
denotes cast-inserting translation.
The term of the form
\mathit{f}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
is a cast of
\mathit{f}
from
type
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
to
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and appears where typechecking was loosened due to type
consistency.
1
1

1

The symbol
\ell
is called a blame label and is used to identify a
cast. Following
Siek
et al. 2015a
, we use
\mathit{f}
for the intermediate language and save
e
for the surface language ITGL.

In these examples, actual arguments
3
and

true
are cast to
\star
and
\mathit{x}
of type
\star
is cast to

int
before being passed to
+
, which expects an integer.
In what follows, a sequence of casts
(f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
is often abbreviated to

f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
.

The former term evaluates to
5
whereas the latter to an uncatchable
exception, called blame
(
Findler and
Felleisen 2002
)
,
\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
indicating that the cast on
\mathit{x}
fails:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})+2)\,(3:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)&\longmapsto&(3:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})+2\\
&\longmapsto&3+2\\
&\longmapsto&5\\[3.00003pt]
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})+2)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)&\longmapsto&(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})+2\\
&\longmapsto&\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}+2\\
&\longmapsto&\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}\end{array}

The terms
3:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star
and
\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star
are values
in the blame calculus and they can be understood as an integer and
Boolean, respectively, tagged with its type. Being values, they are
passed to the function as they are. The cast from
\star
to

int
investigates if the tag of the target is
int
; if it
is, the tag is removed and the untagged integer is passed to
+
;
otherwise, blame is raised with information on which cast has failed.

1.2.
Type Inference for Gradual Typing

Type inference (a.k.a. type reconstruction) for languages with the
dynamic type has been studied.
Siek and
Vachharajani 2008
proposed
a unification-based type inference algorithm for a gradually typed
language with simple types.
Garcia and Cimini 2015
later
proposed a type inference algorithm with a principal type property for
the Implicitly Typed Gradual Language (ITGL) with and without
let-polymorphism. More recently,
Xie
et al. 2018

studied an extension of the Odersky–Läufer type
system for higher-rank polymorphism
(
Odersky and
Läufer 1996
)

with the dynamic type and bidirectional algorithmic typing for it.
Also,
Henglein and
Rehof 1995
studied a very close
problem of translation from an untyped functional language to an
ML-like language with coercions
(
Henglein 1994
)
, using a constraint-based
type inference.

The key idea in Garcia and Cimini’s work (inherited by Xie et al.) is to infer only

static
types—that is, types not containing
\star
—for
where type annotations are omitted. For example, for

(\lambda\mathit{x}.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y}),

the type inference algorithm outputs the following fully annotated term:

e=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y}).

The idea of inferring only static types is significant for the
principal type property because it excludes terms like

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})
,
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})
, and

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})
, which are all well typed in the gradual
type system but not obtained by applying type substitution to

e
.
Based on this idea, they showed that the ITGL enjoys the principal type property, which means that if there are type
annotations
to make a given term well typed, the type
inference succeeds and its output subsumes all other type annotations that make it well typed—in the sense that
they are obtained by applying some type substitution.

1.3.
Incoherence Problem

Unlike ordinary typed
\lambda
-calculi, however, the behavior of a
term depends on concrete types chosen for missing type annotations.
For example, for the following term

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}.\,\mathit{y})

it is appropriate to recover any static type
\mathit{T}
for
\mathit{y}
if we
are interested only in obtaining a well-typed term because

\star\sim\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{T}
but the evaluation of the resulting term
significantly differs depending on the choice for
\mathit{T}
. To see
this, let’s translate
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\mathit{y})
(of type
\star
).
It is translated to

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{T}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star)

regardless of
\mathit{T}
. If
\mathit{T}=\textsf{{int}}
, then
it reduces to value
2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y}):\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star)\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})):\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\\
\longmapsto&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})\,2):\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\\
\longmapsto&2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\end{array}

but, if
\mathit{T}=\textsf{{bool}}
, it reduces to
\textsf{{blame}}\,\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{bool}}.\,\mathit{y}):\textsf{{bool}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star)\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{bool}}.\,\mathit{y})\,\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\textsf{{bool}})\!$}\!):\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\\
\longmapsto&\textsf{{blame}}\,\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\end{array}

(the shaded subterm is the source of blame and the blame label

\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
, which is the negation of
\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
, means that a functional
cast labeled
\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}
has failed due to type mismatch on the argument,
not on the return value).
Xie
et al. 2018
face the
same problem in a slightly different setting of a higher-rank
polymorphic type system with the dynamic type and point out that their
type system is not

coherent

(
Breazu-Tannen et al. 1991
)
in the
sense that the run-time behavior of the same source program depends on
the particular choice of types.

Garcia and Cimini 2015
do not clearly discuss how to deal
with this problem. Given term
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}.\,\mathit{y})
, their type
reconstruction algorithm outputs
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y})
, where

\mathit{Y}
is a type variable that is left undecided and they suggest
those undecided variables be replaced by type parameters but their
semantics is left unclear. One possibility would be to understand
type parameters as distinguished base types (without constants) but it
would also make the execution fail because
\textsf{{int}}\neq\mathit{Y}
.
The problem here is that the only choice for
\mathit{T}
that makes execution
successful is
int
but it is hard to see statically.

An alternative, which is close to what
Henglein and
Rehof 1995
do and
also what
Xie
et al. 2018
suggest,
is to substitute the dynamic type
\star
for these undecided
type variables. If we replace
\mathit{Y}
with
\star
in the example
above, we will get

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star)\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\\
\longmapsto&2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\ .\end{array}

As far as this example is concerned, substitution of
\star
sounds
like a good idea as, if there is static-type substitution that
makes execution successful (i.e., terminate at a value), substitution
of
\star
is expected to make execution also successful—this is
part of the gradual guarantee
property
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
.

However, substitution of
\star
can make execution successful, even
when there is
no static type
that makes execution successful.
For example, let’s consider the ITGL term

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2\,\textsf{{true}})\,(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})

where
\textsf{{ if }}e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ then }}e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\textsf{{ else }}e_{{\mathrm{3}}}

requires
e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
e_{{\mathrm{3}}}
to have the same type and
\mathit{b}
is a
(statically typed) Boolean term that refers to neither
\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
nor
\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.
For this term, the type inference algorithm outputs

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2\,\textsf{{true}})\,(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})

and, if we substitute
\star
for
\mathit{Y}
, then it executes as
follows:

\begin{array}[]{cl}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2\,\textsf{{true}})\,(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\star.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\star.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\\
\rightsquigarrow&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\star.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\star.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)\textsf{{ else }}(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star&\text{(if $\mathit{b}\longmapsto^{\ast}\textsf{{true}}$)}\\
\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star&\text{(if $\mathit{b}\longmapsto^{\ast}\textsf{{false}}$)}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}

but, if we substitute a static type
\mathit{T}
for
\mathit{Y}
, then it results in blame
due to a failure of either of the shaded casts:

\begin{array}[]{cl}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2\,\textsf{{true}})\,(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\\
\rightsquigarrow&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\\
&\qquad((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{T}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&(((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{T})\!$}\!):\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{T}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\\
&\qquad(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}2\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{T})\!$}\!):\mathit{T}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star)&\hbox{\multirowsetup\text{(if $\mathit{T}=\textsf{{int}}$)}}\\
\hfill\longmapsto\textsf{{blame}}\,\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\\
\textsf{{blame}}\,\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}&\text{(otherwise)}\end{array}\right.\end{array}

Substitution of
\star
is not only against the main idea that only
static types are inferred for missing type annotations but also not
very desirable from the viewpoint of program evolution and early bug
detection: Substitution of
\star
inadvertently hides the fact that

there is no longer a hope for a given term (in this case

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2\,\textsf{{true}})\,(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
) to be
able to evolve to a well-typed term by replacing occurrences of

\star
with static types
.
This concealment of potential errors would be disappointing for
languages where it is hoped to detect as early as possible programs
that the underlying static type system does not accept after
evolution.

1.4.
Our Work: Dynamic Type Inference

In this work, we propose and formalize a new blame calculus

\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
that avoids both blame caused by wrong choice of static
types and the problem caused by substituting
\star
. A main idea is
to allow a
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
term to contain type variables (that represent
undecided ones during static—namely, usual compile-time—type inference) and defer instantiation of
these type variables to run time. Specifically, we introduce

dynamic type inference
(DTI) into the semantics of

\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
so that type variables are instantiated along
reduction. For example, the term

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star)

(obtained from
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}.\,\mathit{y})
) reduces to

((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y})\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{Y})):\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star

and then, instead of raising blame, it reduces to

((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!.\,\mathit{y})\,2):\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star

by instantiating
\mathit{Y}
with
int
. (Shaded parts denote where
instantiation took place.) In general, when a tagged value

w:\iota\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
(where
\iota
is a base type) meets a cast to
a type variable
\mathit{Y}
, the cast succeeds and
\mathit{Y}
is instantiated
to
\iota
. Similarly, if a tagged function value is cast to

\mathit{Y}
, two fresh type variables
\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
are generated
and
\mathit{Y}
is instantiated to
\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, expecting further
instantiation later.

Unlike the semantics based on substitution of
\star
, the DTI-based
semantics raises blame if a type variable appears in conflicting
contexts. For example, the term

\begin{array}[]{l}(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\\
\quad((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\end{array}

(obtained from

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2\,\textsf{{true}})\,(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
)
reduces in a few steps to

(((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{Y})\!$}\!):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\cdots

which corresponds to
\mathit{x}\,2
in the source term. The cast on

2
succeeds and this term reduces to

((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}2\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)
by
instantiating
\mathit{Y}
with
int
(where the shaded parts are the results of instantiation). Then, in the next step, reduction
reaches the application of
\mathit{x}\,2
to
true
(in the source term):

(((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}2\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})\!$}\!):\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star).

However, the shaded cast on
true
fails. In short,
\mathit{Y}
is
required to be both
int
and
bool
at the same time, which
is impossible. As this example shows, DTI is not as permissive as the
semantics based on substitution of
\star
and detects a type error
early.

DTI is
sound
and
complete
. Intuitively, soundness means
that, if a program evaluates to a value under the DTI semantics, then
the program obtained by applying—in advance—the type instantiation
that DTI found results in the same value and if a program results in
blame under the DTI semantics, then all type substitutions make the
program result also in blame. Completeness means that, if some type
substitution makes the program evaluate to a value, then execution
with DTI also results in a related value. Soundness also means that
the semantics is not too permissive: it is not the case that a program
evaluates to a value under the DTI semantics but no type substitution
makes the program evaluate to a value. The semantics based on
substituting
\star
is complete but not sound; the semantics based on
“undecided type variables as base types” as in

Garcia and Cimini 2015
is neither sound nor complete
(because it just raises blame too often).

We equip
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
with ML-style let-polymorphism
(
Milner 1978
)
.
Actually, Garcia and Cimini have already proposed two ways to implement the
ITGL with let-polymorphism: by translating it to the Polymorphic Blame
Calculus
(
Ahmed
et al. 2011
;
Ahmed
et al. 2017
)

or by expanding
let
before translating it to the (monomorphic) blame
calculus. However, they have left a detailed comparison of the two to
future work. Our semantics is very close to the latter, although we
do not statically expand definitions by
let
. Perhaps surprisingly,
the semantics is not quite parametric; we argue that translation to
the Polymorphic Blame Calculus, which dynamically enforces
parametricity
(
Reynolds 1983
;
Ahmed
et al. 2017
)
,
has an undesirable consequence and our semantics (which is close to the one based on expanding
let
)
is better suited for languages in which type abstraction
and application are implicit.

Other than soundness and completeness of DTI, we also study the gradual guarantee
property
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
for the ITGL. The gradual
guarantee formalizes an informal expectation for gradual typing
systems that adding more static types to a program only exposes type
errors—whether they are static or dynamic—but should not change
the behavior of the program otherwise.
To deal with the ITGL, where bound variables come with optional type
annotations, we extend the notion of “more static types” (formalized
as
precision
relations
\sqsubseteq
over types and terms) so
that an omitted type annotation is more precise than the annotation
with
\star
but less precise than an annotation with a static type.
So, for example,

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})\sqsubseteq(\lambda\mathit{x}.\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})\sqsubseteq(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y}).

Intuitively, omitted type annotations are considered (fresh) type
variables, which are less specific than concrete types but they are
more precise than
\star
because they range only over static types.
We prove the gradual guarantee for the ITGL. To our knowledge, the
gradual guarantee is proved for a language with let-polymorphism for
the first time.

Finally, we have implemented an interpreter of the ITGL, including an
implementation of Garcia and Cimini’s type inference algorithm, a
translator to and an evaluator of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
, in OCaml. It
supports integer, Boolean, and unit types as base types, standard
arithmetic, comparison, and Boolean operators, conditional
expressions, and recursive definitions. The source code is available
at
https://github.com/ymyzk/lambda-dti/
.

Contributions.

Our contributions are summarized as follows:

•

We propose DTI as a basis for new semantics of (an intermediate language for) the ITGL, an implicitly typed language with
a gradual type system and Hindley–Milner polymorphism;

•

We define a blame calculus
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
with its syntax, type system, and operational semantics with DTI;

•

We prove properties of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
, including type safety,
soundness and completeness of DTI, and the gradual guarantee;

•

We also prove the gradual guarantee for the ITGL; and

•

We have implemented an interpreter of the ITGL.

The organization of the paper.

We define
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
in Section
2
and state its
basic properties, including type safety and conservative extension, in
Section
3
. Then, we show soundness and
completeness of DTI in Section
4
and
the gradual guarantee in Section
5
.
Finally, we discuss related work in Section
6
and
conclude in Section
7
. Proofs of the stated properties are given in Appendix.

2.
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
: A Blame Calculus with Dynamic Type Inference

In this section, we develop a new blame calculus
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
with
dynamic type inference (DTI). We start with a simply typed fragment and add
let-polymorphism in Section
2.3
. The core of the
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
calculus is
based on the calculus by
Siek
et al. 2015a
,
which is a simplified version of the blame calculus by

Wadler and
Findler 2009
without refinement types. We
augment its type system with type variables in a fairly
straightforward manner and operational semantics as described in the
last section.

Our blame calculus
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
is designed to be used as an
intermediate language to give semantics of the ITGL by

Garcia and Cimini 2015
. We will discuss the ITGL and
how ITGL programs are translated to
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
programs in
Section
5
in more detail, but as far as
the simply typed fragment is concerned it is very similar
to previous work
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
;
Garcia and Cimini 2015
)
.

2.1.
Static Semantics

We show the syntax of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
in Figure
1
. The syntax
of the calculus extends that of the simply typed lambda calculus with the
dynamic type, casts, and type variables.

\begin{array}[]{rlcl}\textrm{Base Types}&\iota&&\textrm{Type Variables}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{X},\mathit{Y}\hskip 18.49988pt\textrm{Blame Labels}\hskip 9.24994pt\ell\\
\textrm{Static Types}&\mathit{T}&::=&\mathit{X}\mid\iota\mid\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{T}\\
\textrm{Gradual Types}&U&::=&\star\mid\mathit{X}\mid\iota\mid\mathit{U}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U}\\
\textrm{Ground Types}&\mathit{G}&::=&\iota\mid\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\\
\textrm{Terms}&\mathit{f}&::=&\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{c}\mid\mathit{op}\,(f,f)\mid\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,\mathit{f}\mid\mathit{f}\,\mathit{f}\mid\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U}\mid\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell\\
\textrm{Values}&w&::=&\mathit{c}\mid\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,\mathit{f}\mid w:\mathit{U}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U}\mid w:\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\\
\textrm{Results}&\mathit{r}&::=&w\mid\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell\\
\textrm{Type Environments}&\Gamma&::=&\emptyset\mid\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{U}\\
\textrm{Evaluation Contexts}&\mathit{E}&::=&\left[\,\right]\mid\mathit{op}\,(\mathit{E},\mathit{f})\mid\mathit{op}\,(w,\mathit{E})\mid\mathit{E}\,\mathit{f}\mid w\,\mathit{E}\mid\mathit{E}:\mathit{U}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\,\mathit{U}\\
\end{array}

Figure 1
.
Syntax of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
.

Gradual types, denoted by
\mathit{U}
, consist of base types
\iota

(such as
int
and
bool
), type variables
\mathit{X}
, the
dynamic type
\star
, and function types
\mathit{U}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U}
. Static types,
denoted by
\mathit{T}
, are the subset of gradual types without the
dynamic type. Ground types, which are used as tags to inject values
into the dynamic type, contain base types
\iota
and the function
type
\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
. We emphasize that type variables are
not
in
ground types, because no value inhabits them (as we show in the canonical forms lemma (Lemma
2
)) and we
do not need to use a type variable as a tag to inject values.

Terms, denoted by
\mathit{f}
, consist of variables
\mathit{x}
, constants
\mathit{c}
,
primitive operations
\mathit{op}\,(\mathit{f},\mathit{f})
, lambda abstractions
\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,\mathit{f}
(which bind
\mathit{x}
in
\mathit{f}
),
applications
\mathit{f}\,\mathit{f}
, casts
\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U}
, and blame
\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
. Since
this is an intermediate language, variables in abstractions are explicitly typed. Casts

\mathit{f}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
from
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
to
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
are inserted when
translating a term in the ITGL, and used for checking whether
\mathit{f}

of type
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
can behave as type
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
at run time. Casts are annotated
also with blame labels, denoted by
\ell
, to indicate which cast has
failed; blame
\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
is used to denote a run-time failure of a
cast with
\ell
. They have

polarity
to indicate which side of a cast to be
blamed
(
Findler and
Felleisen 2002
)
. For each blame label, there
is a negated blame label
\bar{\ell}
, which denotes the opposite side to
\ell
, and
\bar{\bar{\ell}}=\ell
. As we did in the introduction, we
often abbreviate a sequence of casts
(\mathit{f}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{3}}}}

to
\mathit{f}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
.

Values, denoted by
w
, consist of constants, lambda abstractions, wrapped
functions, and injections. A wrapped function is a function value
enclosed in the cast between function types. Results, denoted by

\mathit{r}
, are values and blame. Evaluation contexts, denoted by

\mathit{E}
, are standard and they mean that a term is evaluated from left
to right, under call-by-value.

We show the static semantics of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
in Figure
2
.
It consists of type consistency and typing.

Type consistency rules define the type consistency relation
\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{U^{\prime}}
, a key
notion in gradual typing, over gradual types. Intuitively,
\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{U^{\prime}}

means that it is possible for a cast from
\mathit{U}
to
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
to succeed.
The rules
(
C_Base
) and (
C_TyVar
) mean that a base type and a type
variable, respectively, is consistent with itself. The rules
(
C_DynL
) and (
C_DynR
) mean that all gradual types are
consistent with the dynamic type. The rule (
C_Arrow
) means that
two function types are consistent if their domain types are consistent
and so are their range types. The type consistency relation is
reflexive and symmetric but not transitive.

Typing rules of the
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
extend those of the simply typed
lambda calculus. The rules (
T_Var
), (
T_Const
), (
T_Op
),
(
T_Abs
), and (
T_App
) are standard.
\mathit{ty}(\mathit{c})
used in (
T_Const
)
assigns a base type to each constant
\mathit{c}
, and
\mathit{ty}(\mathit{op})
used in (
T_Op
)
assigns a first-order static type without type variables to each operator

\mathit{op}
. The rule (
T_Cast
) allows a term to be cast to a consistent
type.

Type consistency:
\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{U}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\iota\sim\iota}\ \textsc{{{C\_Base}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathit{X}\sim\mathit{X}}\ \textsc{{{C\_TyVar}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\star\sim\mathit{U}}\ \textsc{{{C\_DynL}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathit{U}\sim\star}\ \textsc{{{C\_DynR}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\sim\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{21}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\sim\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{22}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\sim\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{21}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{22}}}}}\ \textsc{{{C\_Arrow}}{}}}

Typing rules:
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{x}:\mathit{U}\,\in\,\Gamma\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{U}}\ \textsc{{{T\_Var}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:\mathit{ty}(\mathit{c})}\ \textsc{{{T\_Const}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{ty}(\mathit{op})=\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{op}\,(\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\iota}\ \textsc{{{T\_Op}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,\mathit{f}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{T\_Abs}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{T\_App}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{U^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash(\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U^{\prime}}):\mathit{U^{\prime}}}\ \textsc{{{T\_Cast}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell:\mathit{U}}\ \textsc{{{T\_Blame}}{}}}

Figure 2
.
Static semantics of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
.

A type substitution, denoted by
S
, is a finite mapping from type
variables to static types. The empty mapping is denoted by
[]
; and
the composition of two type substitutions
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is by
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\circ S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
Application
S(\mathit{f})
and
S(\mathit{U})
of type substitution
S
to term

\mathit{f}
and type
\mathit{U}
are defined in the usual way respectively.
We write
[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T}}]
for a type substitution that maps type variables

\overrightarrow{\mathit{X}}
to types
\overrightarrow{\mathit{T}}
respectively and
\mathit{f}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T}}]
and
\mathit{U}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T}}]

for
[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T}}](\mathit{f})
and
[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T}}](\mathit{U})
.
Note that the codomain of a type substitution is static
types, following
Garcia and Cimini 2015
,
in which a type variable represents a placeholder for a static type.

As expected, type substitution preserves consistency and typing:

0 (name=Type Substitution Preserves Consistency and Typing,restate=lemTySubstConsistency).

(1)

If
\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{U^{\prime}}
, then
S(\mathit{U})\sim S(\mathit{U^{\prime}})
for any
S
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
, then
S(\Gamma)\vdash S(\mathit{f}):S(\mathit{U})
for any
S
.

2.2.
Dynamic Semantics

We show the dynamic semantics of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
in
Figure
3
. It is given in a small-step style by using two relations over
terms. One is the reduction relation
\mathit{f}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
, which
represents a basic computation step, including dynamic checking by
casts and DTI. The other is the evaluation relation
\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
,
which represents top-level execution. Both relations are annotated
with a type substitution
S
, which is generated by DTI.

Reduction rules:
\mathit{f}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace*{1.5em}}l@{\hspace*{3em}}rcl@{\hspace*{1.5em}}l}\mathit{op}\,(w_{{\mathrm{1}}},w_{{\mathrm{2}}})&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&\llbracket\mathit{op}\rrbracket(w_{{\mathrm{1}}},w_{{\mathrm{2}}})\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_Op}\hfil\hskip 27.74982pt&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,\mathit{f})\,w&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=w]\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_Beta}\\[1.1625pt]
w:\iota\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\iota&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&w\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_IdBase}\hfil\hskip 27.74982pt&w:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&w\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_IdStar}\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace*{1.5em}}l}w:\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{G}&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&w\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_Succeed}\\[1.1625pt]
w:\mathit{G_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{G_{{\mathrm{2}}}}&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\hskip 18.49988pt(\textrm{if }\mathit{G_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\neq\mathit{G_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_Fail}\\
(w_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{21}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{22}}}})\,w_{{\mathrm{2}}}&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&(w_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,(w_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{21}}}}\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}})):\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{22}}}}\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_AppCast}\\
w:\mathit{U}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&w:\mathit{U}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\hbox{\multirowsetup{R\_Ground}}\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil(\textrm{if }\mathit{U}\neq\star,\mathit{U}\neq\mathit{G},\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{G})\hskip 13.87491pt\\
w:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U}&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&w:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U}\hfil\hskip 13.87491pt&\hbox{\multirowsetup{R\_Expand}}\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil(\textrm{if }\mathit{U}\neq\star,\mathit{U}\neq\mathit{G},\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{G})\hskip 13.87491pt\\
w:\iota\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!X&\lx@intercol\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[X:=\iota]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip 9.24994ptw\hfil\lx@intercol&\textsc{R\_InstBase}\\[3.87498pt]
w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!X&\lx@intercol\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[X:=X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!X_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&\lx@intercol w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!X_{{\mathrm{2}}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil(X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textrm{ and }X_{{\mathrm{2}}}\textrm{ are fresh})\hskip 13.87491pt&\textsc{R\_InstArrow}\\
\end{array}

Evaluation rules:
\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f}

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\quad}l@{\qquad}l}\mathit{E}[\mathit{f}]&\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&S(\mathit{E}[\mathit{f^{\prime}}])\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\textrm{if }\mathit{f}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\textsc{E\_Step}\\
\mathit{E}[\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell]&\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\textrm{if }\mathit{E}\neq\left[\,\right]\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\textsc{E\_Abort}\\
\end{array}

Figure 3
.
Dynamic semantics of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
.

2.2.1.
Basic Reduction Rules

We first explain rules from the basic blame calculus, where type
substitutions are empty. The rule (
R_Op
) is for primitive
operations; the meta-function
\llbracket\mathit{op}\rrbracket
gives a meaning to the primitive
operation
\mathit{op}
and we assume that
\llbracket\mathit{op}\rrbracket(w_{{\mathrm{1}}},w_{{\mathrm{2}}})
returns a
value of the right type, i.e., the return type of
\mathit{ty}(\mathit{op})
.
The rule (
R_Beta
) performs the standard
\beta
-reduction. We write

\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=w]
for the term obtained by substituting
w
for
\mathit{x}
in
\mathit{f}
;
term substitution is defined in a capture-avoiding manner as usual.
The rules
(
R_IdBase
) and (
R_IdStar
) discard identity casts on a base
type and the dynamic type, respectively. The rules (
R_Succeed
)
and (
R_Fail
) check two casts where an injection (a cast from a
ground type to the dynamic type) meets a projection (a cast from the
dynamic type to a ground type). If both ground types are equal, then
the projection succeeds and these casts are discarded. Otherwise,
these casts fail and reduce to blame to abort execution of the program
with blame label
\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
(the one on the projection). The rule
(
R_AppCast
) reduces an application of a wrapped function by
breaking the cast into two. One is a cast on the argument, and the
other is a cast on the return value. We negate the blame label for
the cast on the argument because the direction of the cast is swapped
from that of the function cast
(
Findler and
Felleisen 2002
)
. The
rules (
R_Ground
) and (
R_Expand
) decompose a cast between a
non-ground type and the dynamic type into two casts. These rules
cannot be used if
\mathit{U}
is a type variable because type variables
are never consistent with any ground type. The ground type in the
middle of the resulting two casts is uniquely determined:

0 (name=Ground Types,restate=lemGroundTypes).

(1)

If
\mathit{U}
is neither a type variable nor the dynamic type, then there exists a unique
\mathit{G}
such that
\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{G}
.

(2)

\mathit{G}\sim\mathit{G^{\prime}}
if and only if
\mathit{G}=\mathit{G^{\prime}}
.

2.2.2.
Reduction Rules for Dynamic Type Inference

As discussed in the introduction, our idea is to infer the
“value” of a type variable when it is projected from the dynamic
type, as in
w:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{X}
. The value typed at the dynamic type
is always tagged with a ground type and is of the form

w^{\prime}:\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell^{\prime}}\!\star
. If the ground type
\mathit{G}
is a base type, then
the type variable will be instantiated with it.
We revisit the example used in Section
1
and show
evaluation steps below.

\begin{array}[]{ll}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star)\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y})\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{Y})):\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{Y}:=\textsf{{int}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!.\,\mathit{y})\,2):\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\end{array}

The subterm
2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{Y}
on the third line reduces to

2
—this is where DTI is performed; this reduction step is
annotated with a type substitution
[\mathit{Y}:=\textsf{{int}}]
, which roughly
means “
\mathit{Y}
must be
int
for the execution of the
program to proceed further without blame.” As we will explain soon,
the type substitution is applied to the whole term, since the type
variable
\mathit{Y}
may appear elsewhere in the term. As a result, the
occurrences of
\mathit{Y}
in the type annotation and the last cast are
also instantiated (as the shade on
int
indicates) and so

((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y})\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{Y})):\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star
evaluates to

((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})\,2):\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star
in one step.

Now, we explain formal reduction rules for DTI. The rule
(
R_InstBase
) instantiates a type variable
\mathit{X}
with the base
type
\iota
and generates a type substitution
[\mathit{X}:=\iota]
.
The rule (
R_InstArrow
) instantiates a type variable
\mathit{X}
with a
function type
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
for fresh type variables
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.
2
2

2

We use the term “fresh” here to mean that

\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
occur nowhere in the whole program before reduction.

At this
point, we know that
w
is a (possibly wrapped) function, but the
domain and range types are still unknown. We defer the decision about these types by
generating fresh type variables, which will be instantiated in the future evaluation.

Finally, we explain the evaluation rules. The rule (
E_Step
) reduces
the subterm in an evaluation context, then apply the generated type
substitution. The substitution is applied to the whole term

\mathit{E}[\mathit{f^{\prime}}]
so that the other occurrences of the same variables are
replaced at once. The rule (
E_Abort
) aborts execution of a program
if it raises blame.

We write
\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{f_{\mathit{n}}}
if
\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
,

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, …, and
\mathit{f_{{\mathit{n}-1}}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{\mathit{n}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f_{\mathit{n}}}
and

S=S_{\mathit{n}}\circ S_{{\mathit{n}-1}}\circ\cdots\circ S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
(where
n\geq 0
) and similarly for

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{0}}}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{+}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{f_{\mathit{n}}}
(where
n\geq 1
).

One may wonder that the rule (
R_InstArrow
) is redundant
because a term
w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!X_{{\mathrm{2}}}
always reduces to

w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!X_{{\mathrm{2}}}
in the next step. Actually, we could define this
reduction rule in the following two different manners:

•

w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{X}:=\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
; or

•

w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{X}:=\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.

Using these rules does not change the semantics of the language, but
we choose (
R_InstArrow
) for ease of proofs.

We show how the rule (
R_InstArrow
) works using the following
(somewhat contrived
3
3

3

In fact, this term is not in the image of
the cast-inserting translation. An example in the image would be much
more complicated.
) example:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y}+1):\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(3:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{5}}}}\!\star)\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&(w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(3:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{5}}}}\!\star)\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil\textrm{where }w=(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y}+1):\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\lx@intercol\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&(w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!$}\!\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(3:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{5}}}}\!\star)\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil\textrm{where }S=[\mathit{X}:=\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\lx@intercol\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&4:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star\qquad\textrm{where }S^{\prime}=[\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:=\textsf{{int}},\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:=\textsf{{int}}].\end{array}

The type variable
\mathit{X}
is instantiated with
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
when the
value
w
tagged with
\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
is projected to
\mathit{X}
.
Then,
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
are instantiated with
int
by
(
R_InstBase
), as we have already explained, by the time the
term evaluates to the final value
4:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star
.

Perhaps surprisingly, our reduction rules to instantiate type
variables are not symmetric: There are no rules to reduce terms such
as
w:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\iota
,
w:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
,

w:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X^{\prime}}
, and
w:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\mathit{X}
, even though a cast
expression such as
\mathit{f}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star

does
appear during
reduction. This is because a value is not typed at a type variable as we will show in the canonical forms lemma (Lemma
2
)
and we do not need the rules to reduce these
terms. The
\mathit{X}
in
\mathit{f}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
will be instantiated
during evaluation of
\mathit{f}
.

Before closing this subsection, we revisit an example from the
introduction. It raises blame because one type variable is used in
contexts that expect different types:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\;}l}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,w\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil\text{where }w=(\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\lx@intercol\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&w\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&(((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{Y})):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\\
&\qquad(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{Y}:=\textsf{{int}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&(((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,2):\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!\!\rightarrow\!\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\\
\longmapsto^{\ast}&((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}2\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})):\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\\
\longmapsto&\textsf{{blame}}\,\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\end{array}

As for the first example, at the third step, the subterm

(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\mathit{Y})
reduces to
2
by (
R_InstBase
) and
a substitution
[\mathit{Y}:=\textsf{{int}}]
is yielded. Then, by application of
(
E_Step
),
\mathit{Y}
in the evaluation context also gets replaced
with
int
. (Again, the shaded types indicate which parts are
affected.) This term eventually evaluates to blame because the cast

(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\bar{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})
fails. After all, the function

\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
cannot be used as both
\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}
and

\textsf{{bool}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}
at the same time. It is important to ensure
that
\mathit{Y}
is instantiated
at most once
.

2.3.
Let-Polymorphism

In this section, we extend
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
to
let-polymorphism
(
Milner 1978
)
by introducing type schemes and
explicit type abstraction
\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X}}.\,w
and application
\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{T}}]
,
as
Core
-XML
(
Harper and
Mitchell 1993
)
.
(Here, we abbreviate sequences by using vector notations.)
Explicit type abstraction/application is needed because we need type
information at run time.

Our formulation is fairly standard but we will find a few twists that
are motivated by our working hypothesis that
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=w\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}
(in
the surface language) should behave, both statically and dynamically,
the same as
\mathit{f}
where
w
is substituted for

\mathit{x}
—especially in languages where type abstractions and
applications are implicit. Consider the following expression in the
surface language (extended with pairs):

\textsf{{let}}\,g=\lambda\mathit{x}.\,((\lambda\mathit{y}.\,\mathit{y})::\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}\textsf{{ in }}(g\,2,g\,\textsf{{true}})

where
(\lambda\mathit{y}.\,\mathit{y})::\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
stands for ascription, which is translated
to a cast. By making casts, type abstraction, and type application
explicit, one would obtain something like

\textsf{{let}}\,g=\Lambda\mathit{X}\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}\textsf{{ in }}(g[\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{2}}}}]\,2,g[\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{3}}}},\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{4}}}}]\,\textsf{{true}})

in
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
.
Note that, due to the use of
\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
, different type variables are
assigned to
\mathit{x}
and
\mathit{y}
and so
g
is bound to a
two-argument type abstraction. Now, what should type arguments be at
the two uses of
g
? It should be obvious that
\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and

\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
have to be
int
and
bool
, respectively, from
arguments to
g
. At first, it may seem that
\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and

\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{4}}}}
should be
int
and
bool
, respectively, in order to
avoid blame. However, it is hard for a type system to see it—in
fact,
g
is given type scheme
\forall\,\mathit{X}\,\mathit{Y}.\mathit{X}\!\rightarrow\!\star
and, since

\mathit{Y}
is bound but not referenced, there is no clue. We assign a
special symbol
\nu
to
\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{4}}}}
; each occurrence
of
\nu
is replaced with a
fresh type variable
when a
(polymorphic) value is substituted for
g
during reduction.
These fresh type variables are expected to be instantiated by DTI—to

int
and
bool
in this example—as reduction proceeds.

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\textsf{{let}}\,g=\Lambda\mathit{X}\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}\textsf{{ in }}(g[\textsf{{int}},\nu]\,2,g[\textsf{{bool}},\nu]\,\textsf{{true}})\\
\longmapsto&((\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x})\,2,\\
&\qquad(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{bool}}.\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x})\,\textsf{{true}})\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:=\textsf{{int}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&((2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star),(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{bool}}.\,((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\,\mathit{y}):\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x})\,\textsf{{true}})\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:=\textsf{{bool}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&((2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star),(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star))\end{array}

We do not assign fresh type variables when type
abstractions/applications are made explicit during cast insertion. It is because an
expression, such as
g[\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{3}}}}]
, including type applications may be
duplicated during reduction and sharing a type variable among
duplicated expressions might cause unwanted blame.

Syntax:

\begin{array}[]{rlcl}\textrm{Type Schemes}&\sigma&::=&\mathit{U}\mid\forall\,\mathit{X}.\sigma\\
\textrm{Type Environments}&\Gamma&::=&\emptyset\mid\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\sigma\\
\textrm{Type Arguments}&\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}&::=&\mathit{T}\mid\nu\\
\textrm{Terms}&\mathit{f}&::=&\dots\mid\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]\mid\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}\end{array}

Typing rules:
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U}\,\in\,\Gamma\\
\text{For any }\mathit{X_{\mathit{j}}}\,\in\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}},\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{j}}=\nu\,\text{ iff }\,\mathit{X_{\mathit{j}}}\,\not\in\,\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U})\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]:\mathit{U}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]}\ \textsc{{{T\_VarP}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash w_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}\cap\textit{ftv}\,(\Gamma)=\emptyset\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{T\_LetP}}{}}}

Reduction rule:
\mathit{f}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f}

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\hspace*{2em}}l}\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}&\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\textsc{R\_LetP}\\
\end{array}

Substitution:
\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]

\begin{array}[]{rclr}(\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}])[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T_{\mathit{i}}}}](w)\\
&&\lx@intercol\hfil\text{if $|\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}|=|\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}|$ and $\mathit{T_{\mathit{i}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathit{Y_{\mathit{i}}}&\text{$\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}=\nu$ and $\mathit{Y_{\mathit{i}}}$ is fresh}\\
\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.$ }\lx@intercol\\[2.32501pt]
(\mathit{x^{\prime}}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}])[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&\mathit{x^{\prime}}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]&\textrm{if }\mathit{x}\neq\mathit{x^{\prime}}\\[2.32501pt]
\mathit{c}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&\mathit{c}\\[2.32501pt]
(\lambda\mathit{x^{\prime}}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,\mathit{f})[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&\lambda\mathit{x^{\prime}}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,(\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w])&\textrm{if }\mathit{x}\neq\mathit{x^{\prime}},\mathit{x^{\prime}}\,\not\in\,\textit{fv}\,(w)\\[2.32501pt]
(\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}})[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&\lx@intercol(\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w])\,(\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w])\hfil\lx@intercol\\[2.32501pt]
(\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x^{\prime}}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w^{\prime}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f})[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&\lx@intercol\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x^{\prime}}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,(w^{\prime}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w])\textsf{{ in }}(\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w])\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil\textrm{if }\mathit{x}\neq\mathit{x^{\prime}},\mathit{x^{\prime}}\,\not\in\,\textit{fv}\,(w),\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}}}\cap\textit{ftv}\,(w)=\emptyset\lx@intercol\\[2.32501pt]
(\mathit{f}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}})[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&(\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]):\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\[2.32501pt]
\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]&=&\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell\end{array}

Figure 4
.
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
with polymorphic let.

Figure
4
shows the definition of the extension.
A type scheme, denoted by
\sigma
, is a gradual type abstracted over a
(possibly empty) finite sequence of type variables, denoted by

\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}
. A type environment is changed so that it maps variables
to type schemes, instead of gradual types. Terms are extended with

let
-expressions of the form
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}
,
which bind
\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}
in value
w
,
4
4

4

A monomorphic definition
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
(where
\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
is not necessarily a value) can be expressed as

(\lambda\mathit{x}.\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
as usual.

and variables
\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]
, which represent type application if

\mathit{x}
is
let
-bound, are now annotated with a sequence of type
arguments.
5
5

5

A variable introduced by a lambda abstraction is
always monomorphic, so
\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}
is empty for such a variable.

A type argument is either a static type
\mathit{T}
or the special symbol

\nu
.
Type substitution is defined in a capture-avoiding manner.

We adopt value restriction
(
Wright 1995
)
, i.e., the body
of a type abstraction has to be a syntactic value, for avoiding a subtle issue
in the dynamic semantics.
If we did not adopt value restriction, we would have to deal with
cast applications where a target type is a
bound
type variable.
For example, consider the term

\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\mathit{X}.\,w:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}
, which is actually ill
formed in our language because value restriction is violated. It has a cast
with bound
\mathit{X}
as its target type.
The question is how the cast
w:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{X}
is evaluated.
We should not apply (
R_InstBase
) here because the type of
\mathit{x}

would change from
\forall\,\mathit{X}.\mathit{X}
to
\forall\,\mathit{X}.\textsf{{int}}
.
It appears that there is no reasonable way to reduce this cast
further—after all, the semantics of the cast depends on what type is
substituted for
\mathit{X}
, which may be instantiated in many ways
in
\mathit{f}
.
Value restriction resolves this issue:
6
6

6

Making
let

call-by-name
(
Leroy 1993
)
may be another option.
a
cast with its target type being a bound type variable is executed only
after the type variable is instantiated.

The typing rules of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
are also updated. We replace the rule for variables with
the rule (
T_VarP
) and add the rule (
T_LetP
). The rule
(
T_LetP
) is standard; it allows generalization by type variables
that do not appear free in
\Gamma
. Note that it allows abstraction
by a type variable
\mathit{X}
even when
\mathit{X}
does not appear in

\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
. As we have already seen such abstraction can be significant
in
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
. The second premise of the rule (
T_VarP
), which
represents type application, means that extra type variables that do
not appear in
\mathit{U}
have to be instantiated by
\nu
(and
other type variables by static types). The type expression

\mathit{U}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]
is notational abuse but the result will not
contain
\nu
because the corresponding type variables do not
appear in
\mathit{U}
.

The rule (
R_LetP
) is an additional rule to reduce

let
-expressions. Roughly speaking,
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}

reduces to
\mathit{f}
in which
w
is substituted for
\mathit{x}
as
usual but, due to explicit type abstraction/application, the
definition of substitution is slightly peculiar: When a variable is
replaced with a type-abstracted value, type arguments
\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}

are also substituted, after fresh type variable generation, for

\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}
in the value. We formalize this idea as substitution of
the form
\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]
, shown in the lower half of
Figure
4
; in the case for variables,
the length of a sequence
\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}
or
\overrightarrow{\mathit{T_{\mathit{i}}}}
is
denoted by
|\cdot|
.
This nonstandard substitution makes correspondence to usual reduction
for
let
(that is,
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=w\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}\longrightarrow\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=w]
)
easier to see. Other reduction and evaluation rules, including
(
R_InstBase
) and (
R_InstArrow
), remain unchanged.

2.4.
Discussion about the semantics of let-polymorphism

Before proceeding further, we give a brief comparison with

Garcia and Cimini 2015
, who have suggested that the
Polymorphic Blame Calculus
(PBC)
(
Ahmed
et al. 2011
;
Ahmed
et al. 2017
)

can be used to give the semantics of
let
in the ITGL. Using the
PBC-style semantics for type abstraction/application would, however,
raise more of blame—because the PBC enforces parametricity at run
time—even when we just give subterms names by
let
.

The difference between our approach and the PBC-based one
is exemplified by the following program in the ITGL:

(\lambda\mathit{x}.\,1+((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,\mathit{x}))\,2

This program is translated into
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
and evaluated as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\lambda\mathit{x}.\,1+((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,\mathit{x}))\,2&\rightsquigarrow&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(\mathit{x}:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}}))\,2\\
&\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&3\\
\end{array}

This term evaluates to a constant
3
, as expected.
Next, let us rewrite this program so that it uses
let
to give
a name to the function
\lambda\mathit{x}.\,1+((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,\mathit{x})
. Then, this
program is translated into
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
and evaluated as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\textsf{{let}}\,g=\lambda\mathit{x}.\,1+((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,\mathit{x})\textsf{{ in }}g\,2\\
\rightsquigarrow&\textsf{{let}}\,g=\Lambda\mathit{X}.\,\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})\textsf{{ in }}g[\textsf{{int}}]\,2\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(\mathit{x}:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}}))\,2\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&3\\
\end{array}

This program also evaluates to
3
. Notice that
g
is given
a polymorphic type scheme
\forall\,\mathit{X}.\mathit{X}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}
because
\mathit{x}
is
passed to a function that expects an argument of the dynamic type and
there is no constraint on its type. The use of
g
comes with the
type argument
int
so that it can be applied to an integer.
