We show the evaluation sequence of the same term using the
PBC-style semantics of type abstraction and application below:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\textsf{{let}}\,g=\Lambda\mathit{X}.\,\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})\textsf{{ in }}g[\textsf{{int}}]\,2\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&(\Lambda\mathit{X}.\,\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}}))[\textsf{{int}}]\,2\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!\nu\mathit{X}:=\textsf{{int}}\!$}\!.(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}}))\,2\end{array}

When a polymorphic function is applied to a type argument, a type
binding
\nu\mathit{X}:=\textsf{{int}}
is generated, instead of substituting

int
for
\mathit{X}
. As discussed by
Ahmed
et al. 2011
;
Ahmed
et al. 2017
,
the generation of type bindings is crucial to ensure parametricity
dynamically:
\mathit{X}
behaves as if it is a fresh base type in the body
of type abstraction, so evaluation proceeds as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rll}\cdots&\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}&\nu\mathit{X}:=\textsf{{int}}.1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(2:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})\\
&\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&\nu\mathit{X}:=\textsf{{int}}.1+\!\hbox{\pagecolor{gray96}$\!(2:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})\!$}\\
&\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\end{array}

The cast
(2:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})
fails and this program evaluates
to
\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, instead of
3
! In fact, according to
parametricity, the polymorphic type
\forall\,\mathit{X}.\mathit{X}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{int}}
should
behave the same regardless of
\mathit{X}
and so it should uniformly
return an integer constant or uniformly fail. From the viewpoint of
parametricity, this is completely reasonable behavior.

In general, there is a conflict between polymorphism and blame in the
PBC-based semantics. If
\mathit{X}
were not generalized at
let
in
the example above,
\mathit{X}
would be unified with
int
and the
resulting term

\textsf{{let}}\,g=\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,1+(((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})\,(\mathit{x}:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{int}})\textsf{{ in }}g\,2

would evaluate to
3
without blame. (This translation may be
obtained by adapting the type inference algorithm to minimize the
introduction of polymorphism
(
Bjørner 1994
)
to gradual typing.)
The conflict can be considered another instance of incoherence in the
sense that translations that differ in how
let
is generalized
may result in different behavior.

We expect that our semantics is coherent and that operational
equivalence between
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=w\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f}
and
\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=w]
holds
(whereas it does not always hold in the PBC-based semantics as we saw
above). However, they are achieved by sacrificing (dynamically
enforced) parametricity.

3.
Basic Properties of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}

In
this section, we show basic properties of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
: type safety and
conservativity over the standard blame calculus extended to
let-polymorphism.

We first define the predicate
\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
to mean that
\mathit{f}
diverges.

0 (Divergence).

We write
\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
if there is an infinite evaluation sequence from
\mathit{f}
.

We write
\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{f})
,
\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U})
, and
\textit{ftv}\,(\Gamma)
for the set of
free type variables in
\mathit{f}
,
\mathit{U}
, and
\Gamma
, respectively.
We also write
\textit{dom}\,(S)
for the domain of the type substitution
S
.

3.1.
Type Safety

We show the type safety property
using the progress and preservation lemmas
(
Wright and
Felleisen 1994
)
. First,
we state the canonical forms property below.
As we have already mentioned, this lemma also means that no values are
typed at type variables and this is why type variables are not
ground types.

0 (name=Canonical Forms,restate=lemCanonicalForms).

If
\emptyset\vdash w:\mathit{U}
, then one of the following holds:

•

\mathit{U}=\iota
and
w=\mathit{c}
for some
\iota
and
\mathit{c}
;

•

\mathit{U}=\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
w=\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,\mathit{f}
for some
\mathit{x}
,
\mathit{f}
,
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
, and
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
;

•

\mathit{U}=\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
w=w^{\prime}:\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

for some
w^{\prime}
,
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
,
\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
, and
\ell
; or

•

\mathit{U}=\star
and
w=w^{\prime}:\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star

for some
w^{\prime}
,
\mathit{G}
, and
\ell
.

The progress lemma is standard: A well-typed term can be evaluated one step further, or is a value or blame.

0 (name=Progress,restate=lemProgress).

If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
, then one of the following holds:

•

\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
for some
S
and
\mathit{f^{\prime}}
;

•

\mathit{f}
is a value; or

•

\mathit{f}=\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
for some
\ell
.

The statement of the preservation lemma is slightly different from a
standard one, because our semantics, which is equipped with DTI, may
substitute type variables with some static type during reduction and
evaluation.

0 (name=Preservation,restate=lemPreservation).

Suppose that
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
.

(1)

If
\mathit{f}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
, then
\emptyset\vdash S(\mathit{f^{\prime}}):S(\mathit{U})
.

(2)

If
\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
, then
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f^{\prime}}:S(\mathit{U})
.

The second item (for the evaluation relation) does not require
S

to be applied to
\mathit{f^{\prime}}
in the type judgment (whereas the first item
does) because it is already applied—see (
E_Step
).

Finally, type safety holds in our blame calculus using the above
lemmas. The following statement is also slightly different from a
standard one in the literature for the same reason as the preservation.

5 (name=Type Safety,restate=thmTypeSafety).

If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
,
then one of the following holds:

•

\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{r}
for some
S
and
\mathit{r}
such that
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{r}:S(\mathit{U})
; or

•

\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
.

3.2.
Conservative Extension

Our blame calculus is a conservative extension of the standard simply typed
blame calculus
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
extended to let-polymorphism
(we call it
\lambda_{\textsf{B}}
).
We can obtain

\lambda_{\textsf{B}}
by disallowing free type variables and removing reduction
rules (
R_InstBase
) and (
R_InstArrow
). We denote judgments
and relations for this sublanguage by subscripting symbols with
B
;
we also omit type substitutions on the reduction/evaluation relations of
\lambda_{\textsf{B}}

because they are always empty. So, we write
\mathit{f}\,\longrightarrow_{\textsf{{B}}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
for reduction and
\mathit{f}\,\longmapsto_{\textsf{{B}}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
for
evaluation. Then, it is easy to show that
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
is a
conservative extension of
\lambda_{\textsf{B}}
in the following sense.

6 (name=Conservative Extension,restate=thmConservativeExtension).

Suppose that
\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{f})=\emptyset
and
\mathit{f}
does not contain
\nu
.

(1)

\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{r}
if and only if
\mathit{f}\,\longmapsto_{\textsf{{B}}}^{\ast}\,\mathit{r}
.

(2)

\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
if and only if
\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow_{\textsf{{B}}}
.

4.
Soundness and Completeness of Dynamic Type Inference

In this section, we show soundness and completeness of DTI; we
formalize these properties by comparing evaluation of a given term
under
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
and its instances of type substitution in

\lambda_{\textsf{B}}
.

Soundness of
static
type inference means that, if the
type inference algorithm succeeds, the program is well typed (under
the reconstructed type annotation). In DTI, the success of type inference
and the reconstructed type annotation roughly correspond to normally
terminating evaluation and type substitution obtained through
evaluation, respectively. So, soundness of DTI means that, if
evaluation of a program normally terminates at value
w
with a
type substitution (yielded by DTI), then applying the type
substitution
before
evaluation also makes evaluation normally terminate
at the same value.

We state the soundness property below. In addition to normally terminating
programs, we can show similar results for programs that abort by blame
(the second item) or diverge (the third item). The second item means
that, if a well-typed term evaluates to blame with DTI, then
no

substitution
S^{\prime}
can help
S^{\prime}(\mathit{f})
avoid blame. In other
words, DTI avoids blame as much as possible.

1 (name=Soundness of Dynamic Type Inference,restate=thmSoundness).

Suppose
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
.

(1)

If
\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{r}
,
then, for any
S^{\prime}
such that
\textit{ftv}\,(S^{\prime}(S(\mathit{f})))=\emptyset
,

S^{\prime}(S(\mathit{f}))\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime\prime}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,S^{\prime}(\mathit{r})

for some
S^{\prime\prime}
.

(2)

If
\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
,
then, for any
S^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime}(\mathit{f})\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime\prime}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell^{\prime}
for some

S^{\prime\prime}
and
\ell^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
, then,
for any
S
such that
\textit{ftv}\,(S(\mathit{f}))=\emptyset
, either

S(\mathit{f})\!\Uparrow
or

S(\mathit{f})\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
for some
\ell
and
S^{\prime}
.

We state a main lemma to prove Theorem
1
(1) below.

0 (name=,restate=lemSoundnessResult).

If

\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\uplus S_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{r}
and

\textit{dom}\,(S_{{\mathrm{1}}})\subseteq\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{f})
,
then

S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{f})\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{r}
for some
S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that

\textit{dom}\,(S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}})\subseteq\textit{dom}\,(S_{{\mathrm{2}}})
and
\textit{ftv}\,(S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{X}))\cap\textit{dom}\,(S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}})=\emptyset

for any
\mathit{X}\,\in\,\textit{dom}\,(S_{{\mathrm{1}}})
.

Here,
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\uplus S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is a type substitution generated by DTI.
7
7

7

The concatenation
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\uplus S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
of
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is defined only if

\textit{dom}\,(S_{{\mathrm{1}}})
and
\textit{dom}\,(S_{{\mathrm{2}}})
are disjoint and it maps
\mathit{X}
to

S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{X})
if
\mathit{X}\,\in\,\textit{dom}\,(S_{{\mathrm{1}}})
and to
S_{{\mathrm{2}}}(\mathit{X})
if
\mathit{X}\,\in\,\textit{dom}\,(S_{{\mathrm{2}}})
.

Since fresh
type variables are generated during reduction, we split the type
substitution into two parts:
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
for type variables that appear
in
\mathit{f}
and
S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
for generated type variables. The conditions
on the type substitution
S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
mean that
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{f})
may generate
fewer type variables than
\mathit{f}
and the domain of
S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is fresh
(with respect to
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{f})
). The statement is similar to
Theorem
1
(1) but additional conditions make proof by
induction on the number of evaluation steps work.

We cannot say much about diverging programs—Theorem
1
(3)
means that, if a well-typed program diverges, then no type
substitution
S
makes
S(\mathit{f})
normally terminating. One may
expect a stronger property that, if evaluation with DTI diverges, then
without DTI it also diverges (after applying some type substitution that
instantiates all type variables); but actually it is not the case.

3 (name=,restate=thmDivergence).

There exists
\mathit{f}
such that (1)
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
and (2)

\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
and (3) for any
S
such that

\textit{ftv}\,(S(\mathit{f}))=\emptyset
, it holds that
S(\mathit{f})\,\longmapsto_{\textsf{{B}}}^{\ast}\,\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
for
some
\ell
.

We can show that the term
\mathit{f}=((\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,(\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\mathit{x}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star)):\mathit{X}\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,((\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star)
witnesses this theorem.
This term is essentially the combinator

\Omega=(\lambda\mathit{x}.\,\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x})\,(\lambda\mathit{x}.\,\mathit{x}\,\mathit{x})
, for which we would need recursive
types to give a type (if we did not use the dynamic type).
Correspondingly, evaluation of
\mathit{f}
diverges while generating type
substitutions and fresh type variables
infinitely often
:

\mathit{X}
is instantiated to
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
by (
R_InstArrow
),
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{1}}}}

is instantiated to
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{4}}}}
,
\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{3}}}}
is instantiated to

\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{5}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X_{{\mathrm{6}}}}
, and so on. On the other hand, if we instantiate the
type variable
\mathit{X}
in
\mathit{f}
with a finite static type without
type variables, evaluation will eventually reach a cast

w:\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\iota
and fail.

Completeness of ordinary static type inference means that, if
there is some type substitution that makes a given program well typed,
the type inference algorithm succeeds and finds a more general type
substitution. A similar property holds for DTI: if there is some type
substitution that makes a given program normally terminating,
evaluation with DTI also results in a related value and the obtained
type substitution is more general. We can also prove that, if there is
some type substitution that makes a program diverge, then evaluation
with DTI also diverges.

The main lemma to prove completeness is below. It intuitively means
completeness for one step: if a term
S(f)
evaluates in one step
and it does not result in blame, then the original, uninstantiated
term
f
also evaluates with DTI.

The assumption “it does not result in blame”, which means that the
first type substitution is a good one, is crucial.

0 (name=,restate=lemCompletenessValueEvalStep).

If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
and
S(\mathit{f})\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}}
and
\mathit{f^{\prime}}\,\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 25.72433pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{0}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 25.72433pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{0}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 22.5636pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{0}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 22.5636pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{0}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}}}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
for any
S_{{\mathrm{0}}}
and
\ell
,
then
\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime\prime}}
and
S^{\prime\prime}(\mathit{f^{\prime\prime}})=\mathit{f^{\prime}}

for some
S^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mathit{f^{\prime\prime}}
.

We state the completeness property of DTI as follows:

5 (name=Completeness of Dynamic Type Inference,restate=thmCompleteness).

Suppose
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
.

(1)

If
S(\mathit{f})\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,w
,
then
\mathit{f}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime\prime}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,w^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime\prime}(w^{\prime})=w

for some
w^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime\prime}
, and
S^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

(2)

If
S(\mathit{f})\!\Uparrow
, then
\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
.

In the first item,
S^{\prime\prime}
is required to relate the two values
because DTI may still leave some type variables uninstantiated. In
fact,
S^{\prime\prime}
witnesses that
S^{\prime}
is more general than
S
.

Remark:

As we discussed in
Section
1.3
, an alternative to DTI, which is to
substitute
\star
for type variables that type inference left
uninstantiated, breaks the following property, which could be viewed
as soundness:

If
\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
,
S
substitutes
\star
for all type
variables in
\mathit{f}
, and
S(\mathit{f})\,\longmapsto_{\textsf{{B}}}^{\ast}\,w
, then there is some

static
type substitution
S^{\prime}
such that
S^{\prime}(\mathit{f})\,\longmapsto_{\textsf{{B}}}^{\ast}\,w^{\prime}

for some
w^{\prime}
.

The term

\begin{array}[]{l}(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(((\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star))\\
\quad((\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\lambda\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\textsf{{ if }}\mathit{b}\textsf{{ then }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\textsf{{ else }}\mathit{y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{5}}}}\!\star)\end{array}

is a counterexample. However, completeness would be satisfied. The
semantics where uninstantiated type variables are regarded as
distinguished base types, as in
Garcia and Cimini 2015
,
would satisfy neither the second item of soundness nor completeness.

5.
The Gradual Guarantee

The gradual guarantee
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
, a property capturing the
essence of gradual code evolution, is one of the important criteria for
gradually typed languages.
It formalizes the intuition that, in a gradual type
system, giving more “precise” type annotations can find more typing
errors either statically or dynamically but otherwise does not change the behavior
of programs. In a simple setting, a type is more precise than
another, if the former is obtained by replacing some occurrences of

\star
by other types. For example,
\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}
is more precise
than
\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
. The statement consists of two parts: one about
typeability (the
static
gradual guarantee) and the other about
evaluation (the
dynamic
gradual guarantee).

The static gradual guarantee states that, if term
e
is well
typed, then so is a term that is the same as
e
except that it is
less precisely annotated.

The dynamic gradual guarantee ensures that a more precisely annotated program
and a less precisely annotated one behave in the same way as far as the more
precisely annotated program does not raise blame.
For example, let us consider the following gradually typed program (which is
similar to the example used in Section
1
):

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})

This program is translated into a term in the blame calculus and evaluates to
value
2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
.
On one hand, we can obtain a less precisely annotated program by replacing the
type annotation
\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
for variable
\mathit{x}
with
\star
, which is the least
precise type.

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\star.\,\mathit{y})

This program evaluates to the same value
2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
as the gradual
guarantees expects.
On the other hand, we can get a more precisely annotated program by replacing
the type annotation
\star
for variable
\mathit{y}
with
int
.

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,\mathit{y})

Then, it also evaluates to the same value
2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
.
However, more precisely annotated programs may raise blame even if less
precisely annotated ones do not.
For example, let us consider the case that
\mathit{y}
in the above program is given
a wrong type annotation, e.g.,
bool
.

(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star.\,\mathit{x}\,2)\,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{bool}}.\,\mathit{y})

Then, it raises blame because the evaluation triggers a sequence of casts

2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\textsf{{bool}}
, which fails.

In the rest of this section,
we show that the ITGL satisfies both the static and dynamic gradual guarantee.
We extend the statements to take static/dynamic type inference into
account: For example, the statement of the static gradual guarantee
becomes that, given
e
, if static
type inference succeeds, then for any less precisely annotated
term
e^{\prime}
static type inference also succeeds.
To state formally, we first introduce the ITGL and translation of ITGL terms to

\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
terms.
Then, the notion of “more (or less) precisely annotated” ITGL terms
is formalized by a
precision
relation over the ITGL terms.
Using the precision, we show the static gradual guarantee.
The dynamic gradual guarantee is proved by giving a precision relation also for

\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
and showing the two properties: (1) the translation results of
precision-related ITGL terms are precision-related; and (2) precision-related

\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
terms behave equivalently if the more precisely annotated term does
not raise blame.

5.1.
ITGL: the Implicitly Typed Gradual Language

5.1.1.
Definition

This section reviews the definition of the ITGL
(
Garcia and Cimini 2015
)
.

Syntax:

Terms

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle::=\mathit{x}\mid\mathit{c}\mid\mathit{op}\,(e,e)\mid\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,e\mid\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e\mid e\,e\mid\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v\textsf{{ in }}e

Values

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle::=\mathit{c}\mid\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,e\mid\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e

Typing rules:
\Gamma\vdash e:\mathit{U}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U}\,\in\,\Gamma\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}:\mathit{U}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T_{\mathit{i}}}}]}\ \textsc{{{IT\_VarP}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:\mathit{ty}(\mathit{c})}\ \textsc{{{IT\_Const}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{ty}(\mathit{op})=\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sim\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sim\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{op}\,(e_{{\mathrm{1}}},e_{{\mathrm{2}}}):\iota}\ \textsc{{{IT\_Op}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash e:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,e:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{IT\_AbsE}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}\vdash e:\mathit{U}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e:\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U}}\ \textsc{{{IT\_AbsI}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\triangleright\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sim\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,e_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}}\ \textsc{{{IT\_App}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash v_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash e_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}=\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\setminus(\textit{ftv}\,(\Gamma)\cup\textit{ftv}\,(v_{{\mathrm{1}}}))\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}e_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{IT\_LetP}}{}}}

Cast insertion rules:
\Gamma\vdash e\rightsquigarrow\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U}\,\in\,\Gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\text{For any }\mathit{X_{\mathit{j}}}\,\in\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}},\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{j}}=\nu\,\text{ iff }\,\mathit{X_{\mathit{j}}}\,\not\in\,\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U})\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}\rightsquigarrow\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]:\mathit{U}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]}\ \textsc{{{CI\_VarP}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}\rightsquigarrow\mathit{c}:\mathit{ty}(\mathit{c})}\ \textsc{{{CI\_Const}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rightsquigarrow f_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rightsquigarrow f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\mathit{ty}(\mathit{op})=\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sim\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sim\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathit{op}\,(e_{{\mathrm{1}}},e_{{\mathrm{2}}})\rightsquigarrow\mathit{op}\,(f_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}},f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}):\iota}\ \textsc{{{CI\_Op}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{T}\vdash e\rightsquigarrow f:\mathit{U}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e\rightsquigarrow\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,f:\mathit{T}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U}}\ \textsc{{{CI\_AbsI}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash e\rightsquigarrow f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,e\rightsquigarrow\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{CI\_AbsE}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rightsquigarrow f_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rightsquigarrow f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\triangleright\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sim\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rightsquigarrow(f_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}})\,(f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}):\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}}\ \textsc{{{CI\_App}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash v_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rightsquigarrow w_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{Y_{\mathit{j}}}}.\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rightsquigarrow f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}=\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\setminus(\textit{ftv}\,(\Gamma)\cup\textit{ftv}\,(v_{{\mathrm{1}}}))\hskip 9.24994pt\overrightarrow{\mathit{Y_{\mathit{j}}}}=\textit{ftv}\,(w_{{\mathrm{1}}})\setminus(\textit{ftv}\,(\Gamma)\cup\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\cup\textit{ftv}\,(v_{{\mathrm{1}}}))\\
\end{array}}{\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rightsquigarrow\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}\overrightarrow{\mathit{Y_{\mathit{j}}}}.\,w_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{CI\_LetP}}{}}}

Figure 5
.
The ITGL.

Figure
5
shows the syntax and the typing rules of the ITGL and
the cast insertion rules to translate the ITGL to
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
.
The syntax is from the standard lambda calculus, except that
it provides two kinds of abstractions: one is
\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,e
, where the type of
argument
\mathit{x}
is given as
\mathit{U}
explicitly, and the other is
\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e
,
where the type of
\mathit{x}
is implicit.
A
let
-expression
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v\textsf{{ in }}e
allows
v
to be polymorphic in

e
; type variables to be generalized are implicit in terms of
the ITGL, while they are explicit in
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
.

The typing rules are essentially the same as what is called schematic
typing in
Garcia and Cimini 2015
modulo a few minor
adaptations.
The rules (
IT_VarP
) and (
IT_AbsI
) require implicit types to be static.
The rule (
IT_Op
) says that types of arguments have to be consistent with the
argument types of
\mathit{op}
.
The rule (
IT_App
) for application
e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is standard in gradual
typing
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
.
The type
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
of
e_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is consistent with function type
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}

obtained by operation
\triangleright
, which is defined as

\begin{array}[]{ccc@{\qquad\qquad}ccc}\star&\triangleright&\star\!\rightarrow\!\star&\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}&\triangleright&\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}},\end{array}

and the type
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
of
e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
has to be consistent with the argument type of

\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{12}}}}
.
The rule (
IT_Let
) is standard in languages with
let-polymorphism
(
Milner 1978
)
except the condition on type variables to
be generalized.
We do not allow type variables that appear in type annotations of
v
to be
generalized because allowing it invalidates the property that

\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v\textsf{{ in }}e
is well typed if and only if so is

e[\mathit{x}:=v]
(if
e
refers to
\mathit{x}
).
For example, let us consider ITGL program

\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,\mathit{y}\textsf{{ in }}(\mathit{x}\,2,\mathit{x}\,\textsf{{true}}).

This program would be well typed if
\mathit{X}
could be generalized.
However, the result of expanding let

(\mathit{x}\,2,\mathit{x}\,\textsf{{true}})[\mathit{x}:=\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,\mathit{y}]=((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,\mathit{y})\,2,(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,\mathit{y})\,\textsf{{true}})

is not well typed because
\mathit{X}
could not be instantiated in two ways:

int
and
bool
.
Our restriction to generalizable type variables rejects not only the latter but
also the former; OCaml seems to adopt the same strategy (such type variables
are generalized only at the top level).

Translation
\Gamma\vdash e\rightsquigarrow\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
of ITGL term
e
of type
\mathit{U}
to

\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
term
\mathit{f}
is achieved by inserting casts and making implicit
type information explicit.
The cast insertion rules, which are shown in the lower half of
Figure
5
, are standard
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
except
(
CI_VarP
) and (
CI_LetP
).
The rule (
CI_VarP
) makes type instantiations explicit in terms.
The side condition is the same as that of (
T_VarP
).
The rule (
CI_LetP
) is similar to (
T_LetP
), but it allows not only type
variables
\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}
that appear in the type
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
of polymorphic value

w_{{\mathrm{1}}}
but also
\overrightarrow{\mathit{Y_{\mathit{i}}}}
that appear only in
w_{{\mathrm{1}}}
—they are implicit in ITGL
terms—to be generalized for identifying
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v\textsf{{ in }}e
with
e[\mathit{x}:=v]

semantically.
For example, let us consider the following ITGL term:

\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\lambda\mathit{y}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}.\,\mathit{z})::\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{y}\textsf{{ in }}(\mathit{x}\,2,\mathit{x}\,\textsf{{true}})

where
e::\mathit{U}
means
(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,\mathit{x})\,e
.
If type variables that appear in only
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
could be generalized,
it would be translated to

\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\mathit{X}.\,\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\mathit{X}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}\!:\!\mathit{Y}.\,\mathit{z}):\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\mathit{y}:\mathit{X}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star)\textsf{{ in }}(\mathit{x}[\textsf{{int}}]\,2,\mathit{x}[\textsf{{bool}}]\,\textsf{{true}})

where
\mathit{Y}
is not generalized.
The evaluation of this term results in blame because the monomorphic type
variable
\mathit{Y}
will be instantiated with different base types
int
and

bool
by DTI.
However, the evaluation based on let-expansion does not trigger blame:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\;}l}&(\mathit{x}\,2,\mathit{x}\,\textsf{{true}})[\mathit{x}:=\lambda\mathit{y}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}.\,\mathit{z})::\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{y}]\\
=&((\lambda\mathit{y}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}.\,\mathit{z})::\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{y})\,2,(\lambda\mathit{y}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}.\,\mathit{z})::\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{y})\,\textsf{{true}})\\
\rightsquigarrow&((\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{int}}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}\!:\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,\mathit{z}):\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\mathit{y}:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\star))\,2,\\
&\quad(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{bool}}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}\!:\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\,\mathit{z}):\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\mathit{y}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star))\,\textsf{{true}})\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:=\textsf{{int}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&((2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star),(\lambda\mathit{y}\!:\!\textsf{{bool}}.\,((\lambda\mathit{z}\!:\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}.\,\mathit{z}):\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\mathit{y}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{4}}}}\!\star))\,\textsf{{true}})\\
\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[\mathit{Y_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:=\textsf{{bool}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}&((2:\textsf{{int}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\star),(\textsf{{true}}:\textsf{{bool}}\Rightarrow^{\ell_{{\mathrm{3}}}}\!\star))\end{array}

By allowing generalization of type variables appearing in not only
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
but
also
w_{{\mathrm{1}}}
(but not
v_{{\mathrm{1}}}
), we achieve the same semantics as let-expansion.

We show that the cast insertion is type-preserving and type safety of
the ITGL.

1 (name=Cast Insertion is Type-Preserving,restate=thmCastInsertion).

If
\Gamma\vdash e:\mathit{U}
,
then
\Gamma\vdash e\rightsquigarrow\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
and
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
for some
\mathit{f}
.

0 (Evaluation of ITGL terms).

We write
\langle\Gamma\vdash e:\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\mathit{f}

if
\Gamma\vdash e\rightsquigarrow\mathit{f^{\prime}}:\mathit{U}
and
\mathit{f^{\prime}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,\mathit{f}
for some
\mathit{f^{\prime}}
.
We also write
\langle\Gamma\vdash e:\mathit{U}\rangle\Uparrow

if
\Gamma\vdash e\rightsquigarrow\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
and
\mathit{f}\!\Uparrow
for some
\mathit{f}
.

0 (name=Type Safety of the ITGL,restate=corTypeSafeITGL).

If
\emptyset\vdash e:\mathit{U}
, then:

•

\langle\emptyset\vdash e:\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997ptw
for some
S
and
w

such that
\emptyset\vdash w:S(\mathit{U})
;

•

\langle\emptyset\vdash e:\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell

for some
S
and
\ell
; or

•

\langle\emptyset\vdash e:\mathit{U}\rangle\Uparrow
.

5.1.2.
Precision

We introduce precision relations for the ITGL to formalize more precise types
and more precisely annotated terms.

Type precision

As we have already mentioned, intuitively, type
\mathit{U}
is more
precise than type
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
if
\mathit{U}
is obtained by replacing some
occurrences of the dynamic type in
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
with other types.
We generalize this notion of precision to type variables.
Type variables are similar to the dynamic type in that type variables that
appear in well-typed terms can be instantiated with any type as values of any
type can flow to the dynamic type.
However, they differ from the dynamic type in that all occurrences of a type
variable have to be instantiated with a single static type.
For example,
\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}
can be considered—and
is
in our
precision—more precise than
\mathit{X}
because we get the former by instantiating

\mathit{X}
with
\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}
, while it cannot be compared with
\mathit{X}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{X}

because
\mathit{X}
is not allowed to be instantiated in two different ways:
int
and

bool
.

Type Precision:
\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\iota\sqsubseteq_{S}\iota}\ \textsc{{{P\_IdBase}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{X}\,\in\,\textit{dom}\,(S)\\
\end{array}}{S(\mathit{X})\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{X}}\ \textsc{{{P\_TyVar}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\star}\ \textsc{{{P\_Dyn}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}}\ \textsc{{{P\_Arrow}}{}}}

Untyped Term Precision:
e\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathit{x}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{x}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_Var}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\mathit{c}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{c}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_Const}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\hskip 9.24994pte^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\end{array}}{\mathit{op}\,(e_{{\mathrm{1}}},e_{{\mathrm{2}}})\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{op}\,(e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}})}\ \textsc{{{IP\_Op}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}e\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}\\
\end{array}}{\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e\sqsubseteq_{S}\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e^{\prime}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_AbsI}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}e\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}\\
\end{array}}{\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e\sqsubseteq_{S}\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,e^{\prime}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_AbsIE}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}e\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}\\
\end{array}}{\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{T_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,e\sqsubseteq_{S}\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e^{\prime}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_AbsEI}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pte\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}\\
\end{array}}{\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,e\sqsubseteq_{S}\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U^{\prime}}.\,e^{\prime}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_AbsE}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\hskip 9.24994pte_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\end{array}}{e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_App}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}v_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}v^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\hskip 9.24994pte_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\\
\end{array}}{\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=v^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}e^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\ \textsc{{{IP\_LetP}}{}}}

Figure 6
.
Precision of the ITGL.

To ensure that all occurrences of a type variable are instantiated in the same
way, we equip type precision
\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}
, which means that
\mathit{U}

is more precise than
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
, with type substitution
S
, which gives
instantiations of type variables in
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
.
The type precision rules, given in the top of Figure
6
, are
standard
(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
except (
P_TyVar
): a base type
is more precise than itself (
P_IdBase
); components of
precision-related function types are also precision-related (
P_Arrow
); and
the dynamic type is the least precise type (
P_Dyn
).
The rule (
P_TyVar
) allows type variables to be instantiated according to

S
.
If we want to relate
\mathit{X}
to itself, we can give substitution
[\mathit{X}:=\mathit{X}]
.
This type precision cooperates well with DTI in the sense that, if the
evaluation of a more precisely annotated term using type annotation

\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}
does not raise blame, then that of a less precisely
annotated one using
\mathit{X}
does not, either, because DTI would
instantiate
\mathit{X}
with
\textsf{{int}}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}
or a less precise static type (such as
\mathit{Y}\!\rightarrow\!\textsf{{bool}}
).

Term precision

Rules of term precision
e\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}
, which means that
e
is
more precisely annotated than
e^{\prime}
, are given in the bottom of
Figure
6
.
All rules but (
IP_AbsIE
), (
IP_AbsEI
), and (
IP_AbsE
) are just
for compatibility.
Lambda abstraction
\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e
is more precisely annotated than
\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,e

(
IP_AbsIE
) because the static type inference gives variable
\mathit{x}
in

\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e
a static type (if any), which is more precise than the dynamic type.
Lambda abstraction
\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{T}.\,e
is more precisely annotated than
\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e

(
IP_AbsEI
) because the static type inference algorithm by

Garcia and Cimini 2015
gives a principal static type to
\mathit{x}
in

\lambda\mathit{x}.\,e
and it should be less precise than
\mathit{T}
under some type
substitution due to principality.
Finally,
\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U}.\,e
is more precisely annotated than
\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U^{\prime}}.\,e^{\prime}
if
\mathit{U}

is more precise than
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
and
e
is more precisely annotated than

e^{\prime}
under
S
(
IP_AbsE
).

5.2.
The Static Gradual Guarantee

0 (Principal Type Inference).

We suppose that there is a partial function
PT(\Gamma,e)
that (1) if

S^{\prime}(\Gamma)\vdash S^{\prime}(e):\mathit{U^{\prime}}
for some
S^{\prime}
and
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
,
produces a pair
(S,\mathit{U})
such that
(a)
S(\Gamma)\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}
and
(b) for any
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
such that

S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\Gamma)\vdash S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(e):\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
, there exists
S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that

S_{{\mathrm{1}}}=S_{{\mathrm{2}}}\circ S
; and (2) is undefined otherwise.

We can obtain a principal type inference algorithm
PT
from

Garcia and Cimini 2015
.
Now, we show the static gradual guarantee for the ITGL.

5 (name=Static Gradual Guarantee,restate=thmStaticGG).

If
e\sqsubseteq_{S_{{\mathrm{0}}}}e^{\prime}
and
PT(\emptyset,e)=(S_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{U})
,
then
PT(\emptyset,e^{\prime})=(S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathit{U^{\prime}})
and
\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mathit{U^{\prime}}

for some
S_{{\mathrm{2}}}
,
S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, and
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
.

5.3.
Precision of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}

As described at the beginning of this section, we show the dynamic gradual
guarantee via reduction of ITGL term precision to
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
term precision.
We denote
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
term precision by

\langle\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}:\mathit{f^{\prime}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle,

which means that
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
term
\mathit{f}
having type
\mathit{U}
under
\Gamma
is
more precisely annotated than
\mathit{f^{\prime}}
having type
\mathit{U^{\prime}}
under
\Gamma^{\prime}
; type
substitution
S
plays the same role as in precision of the ITGL.

Term Precision:

\langle\Gamma\vdash f:\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}:f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U}\,\in\,\Gamma\hskip 9.24994pt\text{For any }\mathit{X_{\mathit{k}}}\,\in\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}},\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{k}}=\nu\,\text{ iff }\,\mathit{X_{\mathit{k}}}\,\not\in\,\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U})\\
\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}.\mathit{U^{\prime}}\,\in\,\Gamma^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{For any }\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{k}}}\,\in\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}},{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{k}}}^{\prime}=\nu\,\text{ iff }\,\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{k}}}\,\not\in\,\textit{ftv}\,(\mathit{U^{\prime}})\\
\mathit{U}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}:=\overrightarrow{{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{j}}}^{\prime}}]\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]:\mathit{U}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}:=\overrightarrow{{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{j}}}^{\prime}}]:\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{j}}}^{\prime}}]\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_VarP}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash\mathit{c}:\mathit{ty}(\mathit{c})\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{ty}(\mathit{c}):\mathit{c}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_Const}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{ty}(\mathit{op})=\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\!\rightarrow\!\iota\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\Gamma\vdash f_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\iota_{{\mathrm{1}}}:f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\Gamma\vdash f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\iota_{{\mathrm{2}}}:f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash\mathit{op}\,(f_{{\mathrm{1}}},f_{{\mathrm{2}}}):\iota\sqsubseteq_{S}\iota:\mathit{op}\,(f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}})\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_Op}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{x}:\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rangle\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}.\,f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_Abs}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\langle\Gamma\vdash f_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\hskip 9.24994pt\langle\Gamma\vdash f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash f_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_App}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\langle\Gamma\vdash w_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sqsubseteq_{[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}]\uplus S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:w^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\hskip 9.24994pt\text{For any }\mathit{X}\,\in\,\textit{dom}\,(S),\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}\cap\textit{ftv}\,(S(\mathit{X}))=\emptyset\\
\langle\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\vdash f_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}.\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rangle\\
\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}\cap\textit{ftv}\,(\Gamma)=\emptyset\hskip 9.24994pt\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}\cap\textit{ftv}\,(\Gamma^{\prime})=\emptyset\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}.\,w^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_LetP}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\langle\Gamma\vdash f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sim\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sim\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash(f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:(f^{\prime}:\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell^{\prime}}\!\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_Cast}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\langle\Gamma\vdash f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}:f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sim\mathit{U}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash(f:\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U}):\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}:f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_CastL}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\langle\Gamma\vdash f:\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\sim\mathit{U^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash f:\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}:(f^{\prime}:\mathit{U^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell^{\prime}}\!\mathit{U^{\prime}})\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_CastR}}{}}}

{\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash f^{\prime}:\mathit{U^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}}{\langle\Gamma\vdash\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell:\mathit{U}\sqsubseteq_{S}\mathit{U^{\prime}}:f^{\prime}\dashv\Gamma^{\prime}\rangle}\ \textsc{{{P\_Blame}}{}}}

Figure 7
.
Term precision of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
.

We show precision rules of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
in Figure
7
.
The rules (
P_Var
), (
P_Const
), (
P_Op
), (
P_Abs
), (
P_App
),
and (
P_Cast
) are similar to the corresponding typing rules of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}

except that they ensure that type information on the left-hand side is
more precise than that on the right-hand side.
The rule (
P_LetP
) relates two let-expressions

\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
and
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}.\,w^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\textsf{{ in }}\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
.
This rule allows polymorphic values
w_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
w^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
to be related under

S
augmented with a type substitution
[\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}:=\overrightarrow{\mathit{T^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}]
, which maps
bound type variables
\overrightarrow{\mathit{X^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}
on the imprecise side to more precise types

\overrightarrow{\mathit{T^{\prime}_{\mathit{j}}}}
on the precise side.
The second premise claims that
S
does not capture bound variables

\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}
; note that free type variables in types to which
S
maps may
appear on only the precise side.
The rules (
P_CastL
) and (
P_CastR
) are given because modifying type
annotations of casts changes casts generated at run time.
The rule (
P_Blame
) represents that a term on the precise side may involve
blame even if one on the imprecise side does not.

5.4.
The Dynamic Gradual Guarantee

Now, we show the dynamic gradual guarantee.

6 (name=Dynamic Gradual Guarantee,restate=thmDynamicGG).

Suppose that
e\sqsubseteq_{S_{{\mathrm{0}}}}e^{\prime}
.
Let

(S,\mathit{U})=PT(\emptyset,e)
and

(S^{\prime},\mathit{U^{\prime}})=PT(\emptyset,e^{\prime})
.

(1)

•

If
\langle\emptyset\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997ptw
,
then
\langle\emptyset\vdash S^{\prime}(e^{\prime}):\mathit{U^{\prime}}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997ptw^{\prime}
and

\langle\emptyset\vdash w:S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{U})\sqsubseteq_{S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{0}}}}S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{U^{\prime}}):w^{\prime}\dashv\emptyset\rangle

for some
w^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, and
S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{0}}}
.

•

If
\langle\emptyset\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}\rangle\Uparrow
,
then
\langle\emptyset\vdash S^{\prime}(e^{\prime}):\mathit{U^{\prime}}\rangle\Uparrow
.

(2)

•

If
\langle\emptyset\vdash S^{\prime}(e^{\prime}):\mathit{U^{\prime}}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997ptw^{\prime}
,
then either (1)

\langle\emptyset\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997ptw
and

\langle\emptyset\vdash w:S_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{U})\sqsubseteq_{S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{0}}}}S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathit{U^{\prime}}):w^{\prime}\dashv\emptyset\rangle

for some
w
,
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, and
S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{0}}}
;
or (2)
\langle\emptyset\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
for some
\ell
and
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

•

If
\langle\emptyset\vdash S^{\prime}(e^{\prime}):\mathit{U^{\prime}}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell^{\prime}
,
then
\langle\emptyset\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
for some
\ell
and
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

•

If
\langle\emptyset\vdash S^{\prime}(e^{\prime}):\mathit{U^{\prime}}\rangle\Uparrow
,
then either (1)
\langle\emptyset\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}\rangle\Uparrow
,
or (2)
\langle\emptyset\vdash S(e):\mathit{U}\rangle\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\textsf{{blame}}\,\ell
for some
\ell
and
S_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

Connection between the gradual guarantee and the soundness–completeness of DTI

The dynamic gradual guarantee states that precise type annotations may find more type
errors at run time but otherwise do not change the behavior of programs.
Perhaps interestingly, the soundness and completeness of DTI also
ensure a very similar property (although it is a property of the
intermediate language, not the ITGL): they state that applying
a type substitution may find more type errors at run time
but otherwise does not change the behavior of programs.
Roughly speaking, the completeness corresponds to the first item of the
dynamic gradual guarantee and the soundness to the second item.
Given the fact that
e=S(e^{\prime})
implies
e\sqsubseteq_{S}e^{\prime}
,
the premise of Theorem
6
is weaker than
those of Theorems
1
and
5
.
However, the conclusions of the soundness and completeness are
stronger than that of the gradual guarantee:
if compared terms evaluate to values, they are
syntactically
equivalent
modulo type instantiations, while in the gradual guarantee
the evaluation results may have different occurrences of the dynamic type.
Moreover, Theorem
1
ensures that, if the RHS
evaluates to a value, then the LHS evaluates also to a value and does
not raise blame.

6.
Related Work

Monotonic reference.

Siek et al. 2015b
study so-called
monotonic references
, which are
an efficient implementation of mutable references for gradually typed languages.
The traditional
approach
(
Herman
et al. 2007
;
Herman
et al. 2010
)
to mutable
references in gradual typing is to create a proxy which performs run-time typechecking on reads
and writes. The proxy-based approach works well theoretically, but it incurs significant run-time overhead even for statically
typed parts in a gradually typed program. Monotonic references preserve a global
invariant that the type of a value in the heap is at least as precise as
the types given to references that point to the value. As a consequence, there is no overhead
for reading and writing through
references of static types
, because
no type (other than itself) is more precise than a static type.
Monotonic references are similar to DTI in the sense that monotonic references
refine types of values in the heap during evaluation whereas DTI refines type
variables to more precise types at run time.

Staged type inference.

Shields
et al. 1998
propose a combination of dynamic
typing and staged
computation
(
Taha and Sheard 2000
;
Davies and
Pfenning 2001
)
in a
statically typed language. In their language, all code values are
given a single code type
\langle\rangle
(without information on the
static type of the code as is typical in type systems for staged
computation
(
Taha and Sheard 2000
;
Davies and
Pfenning 2001
;
Davies 1996
;
Taha and Nielsen 2003
;
Kim
et al. 2006
;
Tsukada and
Igarashi 2010
)
)
and they are typechecked only when it is executed by
eval
.
Such a “typecheck before eval” strategy is called
staged type
inference
. They also propose an
incremental
staged type
inference, where part of typechecking is performed when code fragments
are composed using the quasiquotation
mechanism
(
Quine 1981
;
Bawden 1999
;
Steele 1990
)
;
if an obvious inconsistency (such as applying multiplication to a string
constant) is found, execution results in a special code constant,
which indicates that type checking has failed. This incremental inference
is close to ours in that type variables in code fragments are unified
and instantiated at run time. However, it is limited for code
manipulation. We integrate a similar idea to gradual typing and cast
semantics. Another (somewhat minor) technical difference is that our
semantics does not require full first-order unification at run time:
it is always the case that one of the two types to be unified is a
type variable and there is no need for occur check.

Type inference for gradually typed languages.

Henglein and
Rehof 1995
studied a type reconstruction algorithm
for a language with the dynamic type,
coercions
(
Henglein 1994
)
, and constraint-based
polymorphism. They did discuss the issue of uninstantiated type
variables and propose to replace them with the dynamic type before
running the program. So, their semantics can be too permissive; it
allows successful termination even when there are no static types that
can be substituted for uninstantiated type variables without
causing run-time errors.

Siek and
Vachharajani 2008
propose a type
reconstruction algorithm for a gradually typed language and
Garcia and Cimini 2015
later propose a type inference
algorithm for a very similar language
9
9

9

In fact, their type
systems are shown to be equivalent in a certain sense
(
Garcia and Cimini 2015
)
.
The algorithm in
Siek and
Vachharajani 2008
replaces
uninstantiated type variables with the dynamic type, similarly
to
Henglein 1994
.
ITGL with a principal
type property. The key idea of Garcia and Cimini is to infer only static types for type
variables, which represent omitted type annotations. They have also
discussed how the surface language where type annotations are inferred
can be translated to a blame calculus, where types are explicit not
only in lambda abstractions but also in casts, by showing type-directed
translation into a slight variant
10
10

10

Type variables, which are
bound by type abstraction, and type parameters, which correspond to
type variables left undecided and occur free in a program, are
distinguished in the syntax.
of the Polymorphic Blame
Calculus
(
Ahmed
et al. 2011
;
Ahmed
et al. 2017
)
.
As we have discussed, this translation raises two problems: (1) The
interpretation of type variables left undecided by type inference is
not clear—if they are interpreted as fresh base types, a program may
fail earlier at run time than a type-substitution instance of it; and
(2) the semantics based on the Polymorphic Blame Calculus does not match the
intuition that
\textsf{{let}}\,\mathit{x}=w\textsf{{ in }}f
behaves the same as
f[\mathit{x}:=w]
,
which we believe is desirable especially in languages where type
abstractions and applications are implicit. Actually, Garcia and
Cimini mention that the semantics of the ITGL can be defined by first
expanding
let
and translating into a simply typed blame
calculus—although it would still have the first problem—but
differences of the two translations are not discussed.

Rastogi
et al. 2012
present a flow-based
type inference algorithm for ActionScript. The type system is based
on subtyping rather than polymorphism. Their type inference algorithm assigns
a type without type variables for all type variables, so the problem
we have tackled in this paper does not arise.

More recently,
Xie
et al. 2018
introduce the dynamic
type into the Odersky–Läufer type
system
(
Odersky and
Läufer 1996
)
for higher-rank polymorphism.
They also develop a bidirectional algorithmic type system, which is
used to infer static monotypes for missing type declarations at
lambda abstractions and type instantiations for polymorphic
types. As we have already discussed, they point out that the
interpretation of type variables left undecided by type inference
affects the run-time behavior of a program and suggest to substitute
the dynamic type for such undecided type variables, which is basically the
same idea as
Henglein 1994
and

Siek and
Vachharajani 2008
, although some refinement based on
static and gradual type parameters
(
Garcia and Cimini 2015
)
is
discussed.

Polymorhic gradual typing.

Ahmed
et al. 2011
;
Ahmed
et al. 2017

propose the Polymorphic Blame Calculus, which is based on System F
(
Girard 1972
;
Reynolds 1974
)
.
They introduce the notion of type bindings to enforce parametricity.

Igarashi
et al. 2017
also propose
a polymorphic gradually typed language and a similar polymorphic blame
calculus. As we have already discussed above, it has an undesirable
consequence to implement polymorphic
let
via type abstraction and type
application with dynamic enforcement of parametricity.

Nongeneralizable type variables in OCaml.

In the implementation
ocaml
, a read-eval-print loop for
OCaml, when the type of a given expression (bound by a top-level

let
) contains type variables which cannot be generalized due
to (relaxed) value
restriction
(
Garrigue 2004
;
Wright 1995
)
,
the expression is accepted with those type variables being left
uninstantiated.
11
11

11

OCaml FAQ:

http://ocaml.org/learn/faq.html
(In Standard ML, such an
input requires a type annotation; otherwise it will be rejected.) The
following session with
ocaml
(taken from the OCaml FAQ) shows an
example:

# let r = ref [];;

val r : ’_weak1 list ref = {contents = []}

Here,
’_weak1
stands for a type variable which was undecided by
type inference but cannot be used polymorphically. Such type
variables will be instantiated as the declared variable is used in a
more specific context. For example, the following input enforces that
the contents of
r
have to be an integer list:

# r := 42 :: !r;;

- : unit = ()

Now, the type of
r
changes to
int list ref
by
substituting
int
for
’_weak1
:

# r;;

- : int list ref = {contents = [42]}

This behavior is similar to our DTI in the sense
that type variables left undecided by type inference are instantiated
according to their use. However, instantiation of type variables
is caused by (compile-time) type inference, which takes place before
evaluating every input expression.

7.
Conclusion

We have developed a new blame calculus
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
with DTI, which
infers types for undecided (at compile-time) type variables along
evaluation. We have also extended
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
to let-polymorphism
and proposed a nonparametric semantics, which we argue is better than
the PBC-based
approach
(
Ahmed
et al. 2011
;
Ahmed
et al. 2017
)

for languages with implicit type abstraction and application. Our
calculus can be used as an intermediate language to give semantics to
the ITGL by
Garcia and Cimini 2015
. We have shown the type
safety of
\lambda^{\textsf{DTI}}_{\textsf{B}}
and the soundness and completeness of DTI. We
have also shown the type safety and the gradual guarantee in the ITGL. To
our knowledge, the gradual guarantee for a gradually typed language
with let-polymorphism is shown for the first time. Although we leave
a more formal investigation for future work, we have pointed out a
relationship between the gradual guarantee and the
soundness–completeness property of DTI.

We have implemented a prototype evaluator of DTI, which is used in an
interpreter of the ITGL but leave the study of efficiency issues on
DTI to future work. We are interested in the impact of run-time
overhead incurred by DTI and an efficient implementation to address
it.
Note that not all type variables are subject to dynamic type inference
and some of the run-time type information can be erased
by introducing the distinction between static and gradual type
variables
(
Garcia and Cimini 2015
;
Igarashi
et al. 2017
)
.
In particular, if a program does not use the dynamic type

\star
at all, we expect that a program can be run without passing type
information or instantiating type variables dynamically.
Another direction for efficiency improvement is to integrate DTI with space-efficient cast
calculi
(
Herman
et al. 2007
;
Herman
et al. 2010
;
Siek and Wadler 2010
)
.
The space-efficient calculi make efficient use of the memory space for casts
generated at run time by “merging” two casts into one dynamically.
It is interesting to investigate how to merge casts referring to type variables
effectively.

We expect DTI can be extended to other typing features such as
subtyping. An obvious candidate to apply DTI would be the gradual
extension of higher-rank polymorphism
(
Xie
et al. 2018
)
,
which we also leave for future work.

Acknowledgements.
We would like to thank anonymous reviewers from both PC and AEC for valuable comments and Yuu Igarashi
for the fruitful discussions.
This work was supported in part by the JSPS KAKENHI Grant Number
JP17H01723 (Igarashi)
and ERATO HASUO Metamathematics for Systems Design Project
(No. Grant #JPMJER1603), JST
(Sekiyama).

Lemma A.1 (Weakening).

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
and
\Gamma
does not contain
\mathit{x}
,
then
\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\sigma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the typing derivation of
\mathit{f}
.
∎

Lemma A.2 (Strengthening).

If
\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\sigma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
and
\mathit{x}\,\not\in\,\textit{fv}\,(\mathit{f})
,
then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the typing derivation of
\mathit{f}
.
∎

Lemma A.3.

If
\mathit{U}\sim\mathit{U^{\prime}}
,
then
S(\mathit{U})\sim S(\mathit{U^{\prime}})
for any
S
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the consistency derivation.
∎

Lemma A.4.

If
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U}
,
then
S(\Gamma)\vdash S(\mathit{f}):S(\mathit{U})
for any
S
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the typing derivation of
\mathit{f}
.
∎

Lemma A.5.

If
\Gamma\vdash w:\mathit{U}
and
\Gamma,\mathit{x}:\forall\,\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\mathit{U}\vdash\mathit{f}:\mathit{U^{\prime}}
,
then
\Gamma\vdash\mathit{f}[\mathit{x}:=\Lambda\overrightarrow{\mathit{X_{\mathit{i}}}}.\,w]:\mathit{U^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on the typing derivation of
\mathit{f}
.

Use Lemma
A.4
for the case where
\mathit{f}=\mathit{x}[\overrightarrow{\mathbb{\pdfliteral T\pdfliteral}_{\mathit{i}}}]
.
∎

Case (

):
:

\emptyset\vdash\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{U^{\prime}}
and
\mathit{U^{\prime}}\sim\mathit{U}
.
By case analysis on the reduction rule applicable to
\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:\mathit{U^{\prime}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{U}
.

Case (
R_IdBase
), (
R_IdStar
):
:

We are given

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=w
and
\mathit{U}=\mathit{U^{\prime}}
.
So,
\emptyset\vdash[](w):[](\mathit{U})
.

Case (
R_Succeed
):
:

We are given
w:\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell^{\prime}}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{G}\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,w

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=w:\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell^{\prime}}\!\star
,

, and
\mathit{U^{\prime}}=\star
.

\emptyset\vdash(w:\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell^{\prime}}\!\star):\star
.
By inversion,
\emptyset\vdash w:\mathit{G}
.
So,
\emptyset\vdash[](w):[](\mathit{G})
.

Case (
R_Fail
):
:

We are given

Case (
R_Ground
):
:

We are given
w:\mathit{U^{\prime}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\,\xrightarrow{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,w:\mathit{U^{\prime}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star

T_Cast

\emptyset\vdash[](w:\mathit{U^{\prime}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\mathit{G}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star):[](\star)

A.3.
Divergence

In this section, we do not care about blame labels for ease of proof.
All blame labels are written with
\ell
.

Definition A.1 (Auxiliary Types).

\displaystyle U^{X}

\displaystyle::=\mathit{X}\mid U^{X}\!\rightarrow\!U^{X}

\displaystyle U^{\iota}

\displaystyle::=\iota\mid U^{\iota}\!\rightarrow\!U^{\iota}

Definition A.2 (Order of Type).

•

\textit{ord}\,(\star)=0

•

\textit{ord}\,(\mathit{X})=0

•

\textit{ord}\,(\iota)=0

•

\textit{ord}\,(\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\!\rightarrow\!\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{2}}}})=\textit{ord}\,(\mathit{U_{{\mathrm{1}}}})+1

Lemma A.6.

If

•

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!X.\,(\mathit{x}:X\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\mathit{x}:X\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star)):X\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
, and

•

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
,

then there exist
\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
and
\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
such that

•

\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!X_{{\mathrm{2}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
,

•

\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!X_{{\mathrm{2}}}\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
, and

•

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[X:=X_{{\mathrm{1}}}\!\rightarrow\!X_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\hskip-3.99994pt{}^{\ast}\hskip 1.99997pt\,(\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{f^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
.

Proof.

We are given,

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!X.\,(\mathit{x}:X\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,(\mathit{x}:X\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star)):X\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star
and

•

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
.

,

•

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{12}}}}=(\lambda\mathit{x}\!:\!\star.\,(\mathit{x}:\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\!\rightarrow\!\star)\,\mathit{x}):\star\!\rightarrow\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!X

\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\,\xmapsto{\mathmakebox[0.4em]{}[]\mathmakebox[0.3em]{}}\,(\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{11}}}}\,\mathit{f_{{\mathrm{12}}}}):\star\Rightarrow^{\ell}\!\star
